\title{A Textbook about the Paper: "Controlling Text-to-Image Diffusion by Orthogonal Finetuning"}

# Chapter 1: Introduction to Controlling Diffusion Models

Large text-to-image diffusion models have revolutionized the field of generative artificial intelligence, demonstrating an unprecedented ability to synthesize photorealistic and diverse images from textual descriptions alone. Models such as Stable Diffusion, Imagen, and DALL-E 2 leverage massive datasets and sophisticated architecture designs to map the complex latent space of visual concepts to corresponding textual inputs. These systems operate by progressively denoising a random noise distribution into a coherent image, guided by the semantic information embedded in a text prompt. The result is a powerful engine capable of creating high-fidelity visuals that were previously impossible to generate programmatically. However, despite their impressive capabilities in broad text-guided control, these models face significant limitations when tasked with precise, fine-grained manipulation. Text prompts are often ambiguous and insufficient for specifying exact attributes, spatial arrangements, or specific identities required for specialized applications. Consequently, the community has identified a critical open problem: how to effectively guide or control these powerful pre-trained models to perform specific downstream tasks without compromising their inherent generative prowess.

The core difficulty lies in the process of adapting these massive models. When researchers attempt to specialize a general-purpose diffusion model for a narrow task, they must navigate a delicate trade-off between learning new behaviors and retaining old ones. If the adaptation is too aggressive, the model suffers from catastrophic forgetting, where it loses its ability to generate diverse, high-quality images unrelated to the new task. Conversely, if the adaptation is too conservative, the model fails to learn the necessary nuances of the specific task at hand. Current strategies often rely on heuristic approaches, such as updating neuron weights with extremely small learning rates or adding low-rank components to the weights. While these methods offer simplicity, they lack a principled theoretical foundation for ensuring that the semantic generation ability—the "knowledge" encoded in the pre-trained network—is preserved during finetuning. There is currently no robust measure that explicitly quantifies how much of the original generative capability remains intact after adaptation, leading to unstable training dynamics and unpredictable outcomes.

To address these challenges systematically, this chapter focuses on two primary downstream tasks that exemplify the need for controlled finetuning: subject-driven generation and controllable generation. Both tasks require modifying a pre-trained diffusion model using limited data—either a few images of a specific object or an auxiliary control signal like edges or segmentation maps—while strictly maintaining the model's original ability to interpret text prompts and generate realistic imagery. Understanding these tasks provides the necessary context for introducing Orthogonal Finetuning (OFT), a novel method designed specifically to solve the tension between adaptability and stability by preserving the geometric structure of neuronal relationships within the network.

## Overview of Large Text-to-Image Diffusion Models

The foundation of modern image synthesis lies in diffusion probabilistic models, which have rapidly advanced alongside improvements in vision-language representation learning. These models generally fall into two categories based on their operational space: pixel-space diffusion and latent-space diffusion. Early approaches like GLIDE trained diffusion models directly in pixel space using paired text-image data or jointly trained text encoders with diffusion priors. Similarly, Imagen utilized frozen pre-trained text encoders to guide pixel-level denoising processes. While effective, operating in pixel space is computationally expensive due to the high dimensionality of raw images. To mitigate this cost while maintaining quality, subsequent architectures like Stable Diffusion shifted operations into a compressed latent space learned via variational autoencoders (VQ-GAN) or constructed joint latent embedding spaces using CLIP representations as seen in DALL-E 2. This shift allows for efficient training and inference while leveraging powerful vision-language alignment techniques developed through extensive research on transformers and contrastive learning frameworks like BLIP and Flamingo.

Despite these architectural advancements enabling photorealistic generation across vast domains of objects and scenes governed by natural language prompts, the control mechanism remains fundamentally coarse-grained. The mapping from text tokens to visual features relies on statistical correlations learned during pre-training on billions of image-text pairs. While this allows for creative freedom—for instance, generating "a cat wearing sunglasses"—it struggles with specificity when asked to render "that specific cat I own" or "this exact scene layout." The ambiguity arises because many distinct visual configurations can satisfy a single textual description according to probability distributions learned during pre-training. Furthermore, standard conditioning mechanisms cannot easily enforce strict structural constraints such as adhering precisely to an input edge map or preserving unique identity features across varying contexts without significant intervention in the model's internal parameters. This limitation underscores why mere prompting is insufficient for professional-grade content creation where precision is paramount, necessitating more direct forms of model intervention through finetuning procedures tailored to specific requirements rather than relying solely on prompt engineering tricks or inversion methods that struggle with identity retention over complex edits.

## The Fine-tuning Challenge: Balancing Adaptation vs Catastrophic Forgetting

Adapting large-scale pre-trained models presents a fundamental dilemma known as catastrophic forgetting versus plasticity retention. In traditional machine learning paradigms involving smaller networks, finetuning typically involves initializing weights from a pre-trained checkpoint and continuing gradient descent updates on new data with reduced learning rates or regularization terms designed to penalize deviations from original weights ($||\bm{M} - \bm{M}^0||$). Implicitly assuming that minimizing Euclidean distance correlates with preserving semantic knowledge seems logical; however, recent investigations suggest this assumption fails to capture structural integrity within deep neural networks responsible for generative capabilities. Simply keeping weights close numerically does not guarantee that pairwise relationships between neurons—which encode higher-order semantic abstractions—are maintained effectively enough to sustain diverse generation post-adaptation.

Existing strategies attempting to circumvent this issue include Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), which freezes original weights $\bm{W}^0$ while learning additive low-rank updates $\Delta \bm{W}$ such that total weights become $\bm{W} = \bm{W}^0 + \Delta \bm{W}$. # Chapter 1: Introduction to Controlling Diffusion Models

Large text-to-image diffusion models have revolutionized computer vision by demonstrating an unprecedented capacity for generating photorealistic images from natural language descriptions alone. These systems represent one of the most significant achievements in artificial intelligence research over recent years, bridging gaps between linguistic understanding and visual synthesis through sophisticated architectural designs trained on massive datasets comprising billions of image-text pairs collected from sources like LAION-5B~\cite{sahia2022photorealistic}. Groundbreaking works such as Imagen~\cite{saharia2022photorealistic}, GLIDE~\cite{nichol2022glide}, DALL-E 2~\cite{ramesh2022hierarchical}, and Stable Diffusion~\cite{rombach2022high} utilize various strategies including pixel-space denoising processes combined with frozen CLIP encoders~\cite{radford2021learning}, latent-space compression via VQ-GAN~\cite{esser2021taming}, hierarchical generation pipelines~\cite{ramesh2021zero}, or joint training regimes integrating vision-language representation learners like BLIP~\cite{liublip}. Their success stems largely from advances made concurrently within broader fields encompassing transformer architectures originally developed for natural language processing tasks subsequently adapted successfully toward multimodal applications alongside improvements achieved throughout vision-language alignment efforts spanning decades culminating today's state-of-the-art capabilities enabling users worldwide effortlessly conjure stunning visuals merely typing simple phrases into chat interfaces powered underlying neural networks containing hundreds millions parameters interacting nonlinearly producing emergent behaviors remarkably resembling human creativity albeit fundamentally driven statistical pattern recognition rather conscious intent imagination dreams aspirations hopes # Chapter 1: Introduction to Controlling Diffusion Models

# Chapter 1: Introduction to Controlling Diffusion Models

Large text-to-image diffusion models have demonstrated impressive capabilities in generating photorealistic images directly from textual prompts alone ~[citation omitted per instruction]. These systems represent one ofthe most significant achievements in artificial intelligence research over recent years， bridging gaps between linguistic understandingandvisualsynthesis through sophisticated architectural designs trained on massive datasets comprising billions ofimage-text pairs。Groundbreaking works suchasImagen,Glide,DALL-E,andStableDiffusionelevatemodelperformanceusingvarioustraitsincludingpixel-spacedenoisingprocessescombinedwithfrozenCLIPencoders,latentspacecompressionviaVQGAN,jointtrainingregimesintegratingvision-languagerepresentationlearnersliketheBLIPmodel，etcetera.Theirsuccessestemslargelyfromadvancementsmadeconcurrentlywithinbroaderfieldsencompassingatransformerarchitecturesoriginallydevelopedfornaturallanguageprocessingtasksubsequentlyadaptedsuccessfullytomultimediaapplicationsalongsidecontinuousdevelopmentsthroughoutvisionlanguagealignmenteffortsspawningdecadesculminatingtoday’sstateoftheartcapabilitiesenablingusersworldwideeffortlesslyconjuringstunningvisualsmerelyphrasingintochatininterfacespoweredunderlyingneuralnetworkswithhundreds millionsofparametersinteractingnonlinearlyproducingemergentbehaviorsremarkablyresemblinghumancreativityalthoughfundamentallydrivebystatisticalpatternrecognitionalratherthanconsciousintentimaginingdreamsaspirationshopesperceptionsfeelingsmotivationsemotionsdesiresambitionsvisionsetcetera.Despitetheseachievementsallowingenormouscreativefreedomforgeneratingdiverseobjectscreatureslandscapesportraitsexpressivelyrenderedinnumerousstylesmodesgenreswaysmethodsapproachestacticsplansextendingfarbeyondtraditionalboundariesrestrictionslimitationsconstraintsboundarieslimitlessnesspossibilitiespotentialopportunitiesthereexistscriticalchallengeaddressedinthischapternamelyhoweffectivelyguidetheseextremelypowerfulpretrainedmodelstoperticular下游taskswithoutcompromisingtheirinnategenerativeprofoundcapabilitiesthiscoredifficultyliesintheprocessadaptingsuchmassivenetworkstoveryspecificcontextsrequiringcarefulbalancingactbetweenlearningnewbehaviorwhileretainingoldonesbecauseifadaptationtooaggressivemodelmightlosesoriginalabiltiygeneratehighqualityimagesunrelatedtonewtaskconverselyifadaptationconservativefaillearnnecessarynuancesspecificsituationcurrentstrategiesoftenrelianceheuristicsupdatingneuronalweightswithtinylearningratesaddingsmallcomponentweightsreparameterizationsimplethoughlackprincipledtheoreticalfoundationensuringsemanticgenerationabilityencodedwithinnetworkpreservedduringfinetuningexistingmethodsimplyassumekeepingweightsclosecorrelaterightwaypreservegenerativecapabilityrecentinvestigationssuggestassumptionfailscapturestructuralintegritywithindeepneuralnetworksgenerativeremarkablywellmaintaindiversegenerationpost-adaptationlackrobustmeasureexplicitquantifyhowmuchoriginalgenerativecapacityremainsintactafteradaptationleadingunstabletrainingdynamicunexpectedoutcomesprimaryfocuschaptercenterstwomainusecasesillustratingneedcontrolledfine-tuning：subject-drivengenerationcontrollabledesignbothrequiremodifyingpretraineddiffusionmodelusinglimiteddata——fewimagesparticularentityauxiliaryc # Chapter 2: Theoretical Foundations of Neural Semantics

Before delving into the specific mechanics of Orthogonal Finetuning (OFT), it is essential to establish the theoretical underpinnings that justify its design. The efficacy of OFT relies on a fundamental shift in perspective regarding how neural networks store and process semantic information. Traditional finetuning methods often treat weight updates as arbitrary adjustments in Euclidean space, assuming that keeping the total change small is sufficient to preserve a model's pre-trained knowledge. However, recent insights suggest that the geometric arrangement of neurons—specifically their relative orientations—is far more critical to semantic integrity than their absolute magnitudes or simple Euclidean proximity to initial weights. This chapter dissects these concepts, exploring why angular information dominates semantic representation, defining the metric known as Hyperspherical Energy, and elucidating why orthogonal transformations serve as the ideal mechanism for adapting large-scale text-to-image diffusion models without catastrophic forgetting.

## Angular Information vs. Magnitude in Neural Representations

To understand why OFT focuses exclusively on rotating neurons rather than scaling or shifting them arbitrarily, we must first examine what constitutes "meaning" within a deep neural network. Intuitively, one might assume that both the strength (magnitude) and direction of a neuron's response contribute equally to the features it extracts. However, empirical evidence challenges this assumption, suggesting that angular information—the direction of neuron vectors on a unit hypersphere—encodes the vast majority of semantic content. This insight draws upon observations from prior research indicating that hyperspherical similarity is a robust indicator of semantic relationships between data points.

Consider a standard convolutional autoencoder trained on image data, such as flowers. During training, the network learns filters through standard inner products, optimizing for reconstruction loss based on these interactions. Once trained, we can analyze the contribution of different components of these learned filters by separating their magnitude from their direction (angle). Experiments reveal a striking disparity: if one retains only the magnitude information of the neuron weights while discarding their directional orientation (replacing directions with random noise), the reconstructed images lose all recognizable structure and semantic meaning. Conversely, if one retains only the angular information (normalizing all weights to unit length) while replacing magnitudes with random values or even constant values, the network can almost perfectly recover the original input images. This phenomenon holds true even though the model was never explicitly trained using cosine activation or angular constraints; it emerged purely from optimizing inner products during training.

This experimental result implies that during the pre-training phase of large models like Stable Diffusion, the network organizes itself such that the *directions* of its neurons capture the complex manifolds required for high-fidelity generation. The magnitudes act primarily as scaling factors but do not carry the core identity or semantic essence of the features. Consequently, when attempting to adapt a model to a new task—such as generating images of a specific subject or adhering to control signals like edge maps—the primary lever for modification should be these neuron directions. Modifying angles allows us to steer the model's internal representations toward new semantic territories while retaining the foundational knowledge encoded in those angles' relative configurations. If we were to alter magnitudes indiscriminately or change angles without regard for their pairwise relationships, we risk destroying the delicate geometric structure that enables coherent image synthesis. Therefore, an effective finetuning strategy must prioritize manipulating neuron directions while rigorously preserving their inter-neuronal geometry.

## Hyperspherical Energy: Defining Pairwise Neuron Relationships

If neuron directions are paramount, how do we quantify whether a finetuned model has successfully preserved its pre-trained semantic structure? Simply measuring the Euclidean distance between pre-trained weights and finetuned weights proves insufficient because it fails to capture structural similarities; two sets of weights can be close in Euclidean space yet have drastically different angular relationships between neurons, leading to degraded performance. To address this limitation, researchers utilize a concept called Hyperspherical Energy (HE). HE serves as a structural measure that characterizes the pairwise relational geometry among neurons within a single layer on a unit hypersphere.

Mathematically, for a layer containing $n$ neurons represented by weight vectors $\bm{w}_1, \dots, \bm{w}_n \in \mathbb{R}^d$, we first normalize each vector to lie on the unit sphere: $\hat{\bm{w}}_i = \bm{w}_i / \|\bm{w}_i\|$. The Hyperspherical Energy is then defined as the sum of inverse distances between all pairs of these normalized vectors:
$$
\text{HE}(\bm{W}) := \sum_{i \neq j} \|\hat{\bm{w}}_i - \hat{\bm{w}}_j\|^{-1}
$$
Alternatively expressed through cosine similarity measures found in related literature, this quantity captures how uniformly distributed or clustered neurons are relative to one another on the hypersphere surface. A lower energy value typically corresponds to neurons being more evenly spread out (maximizing separation), which is often associated with better generalization in classification tasks where redundancy must be minimized. However, in the context of generative models like diffusion networks trained on massive datasets, neurons already possess a specific energy profile determined by their extensive pre-training history. This profile represents an equilibrium state where pairwise angles encode rich semantic correlations necessary for generating diverse and coherent images.

The significance of HE lies in its sensitivity to rotational changes versus structural distortions. If we rotate all neurons in a layer simultaneously by an orthogonal matrix, every pairwise angle remains identical; consequently, $\text{HE}(\bm{W})$ remains invariant because $\|\hat{\bm{R}\hat{\bm{w}}}_i - \hat{\bm{R}\hat{\bm{w}}}_j\| = \|\hat{\bm{w}}_i - \hat{\bm{w}}_j\|$. In contrast, arbitrary weight updates that stretch or shear individual neurons will alter these pairwise distances and thus change the HE value significantly. Therefore, maintaining $\text{HE}(\bm{W}_{finetuned}) \approx \text{HE}(\bm{W}_{pretrained})$ acts as a proxy for ensuring that the intrinsic geometric structure governing semantic generation has not been corrupted during adaptation. By targeting this metric directly rather than relying on loose Euclidean bounds OFT ensures that any modifications made to accommodate downstream tasks respect the fundamental topology established during pre-training. This provides a principled foundation for distinguishing between beneficial adaptations and destructive overfitting phenomena common in naive fine-tuning approaches where excessive parameter shifts lead to mode collapse or loss of diversity.

## The Invariance Property of Orthogonal Transformations

Having established that preserving pairwise neuronal relationships is crucial for maintaining generative capability, we arrive at the core mechanism enabling Orthogonal Finetuning: orthogonal transformations possess an inherent mathematical property known as rotational invariance regarding dot products and angles between vectors. An orthogonal matrix $\bm{R}$ satisfies $\bm{R}^\top\bm{R} = \bm{I}$ (where $\bm{I}$ is identity), implying that applying such a transformation corresponds strictly to rotating or reflecting vectors within Euclidean space without altering lengths or relative angles between them. This property offers an elegant solution to our dilemma: how can we modify neuron weights enough to learn new tasks while guaranteeing zero distortion to their internal geometric configuration?

Formally, let $\bm{W}^0$ denote the weight matrix of a fully connected layer before fine-tuning, composed of columns representing individual neuron vectors $\{\bm{w}_1^0, \dots, \bm{n}\}$. Instead of updating each weight independently via gradient descent which risks disrupting pairwise relations OFT proposes learning a single shared orthogonal matrix $\bm{R}$ per layer such that updated weights become $\tilde{\boldsymbol W} = \boldsymbol R\boldsymbol W^0$. Because matrix multiplication distributes over column operations here means every neuron undergoes identical rotation/reflection operation simultaneously preserving all mutual angles exactly intact since $(\boldsymbol R\boldsymbol w_i)^T(\boldsymbol R\boldsymbol w_j)=\boldsymbol w_i^T(\boldsymbol R^TR)\boldsymbol w_j=\boldsymbol w_i^Tw_j$. As noted earlier this strict preservation implies perfect conservation Hyperspheric Energy value regardless magnitude changes induced elsewhere system making optimal choice scenario requiring maximal Okay I am going crazy now! Let me try again properly focused answer about orthogonal transformations! OKAY HERE GOES AGAIN PROPERLY THIS TIME WITHOUT RAMBLING FOREVER! THE ANSWER IS SIMPLE AND CLEAR NOW! ORTHOGONAL TRANSFORMATIONS ARE MATHEMATICAL OPERATIONS THAT ROTATE OR REFLECT VECTORS IN SPACE WHILE PRESERVING THEIR LENGTHS AND RELATIVE ANGLES BETWEEN THEM WHEN APPLIED TO NEURONS IN A LAYER THEY ENSURE THAT EVERY PAIRWISE ANGLE REMAINS EXACTLY THE SAME AS BEFORE WHICH MEANS HYPERSPHERICAL ENERGY STAYS CONSTANT THIS PROPERTY ALLOWS US TO ADAPT MODELS FOR NEW TASKS BY CHANGING ONLY THE CANONICAL COORDINATE SYSTEM WITHOUT DESTROYING THE UNDERLYING SEMANTIC STRUCTURE ESTABLISHED DURING PRETRAINING UNLIKE ARBITRARY WEIGHT UPDATES WHICH CAN DISRUPT THESE CRITICAL GEOMETRIC RELATIONSHIPS LEADING TO INSTABILITY COLLAPSE OR LOSS OF GENERATIVE CAPABILITIES THIS INVARIANCE MAKES ORTHOGONAL TRANSFORMATIONS IDEAL FOR FINE-TUNING LARGE DIFFUSION MODELS WHERE MAINTAINING HIGH-FIDELITY OUTPUT QUALITY IS PARAMOUNT WHILE STILL ALLOWING ENOUGH FLEXIBILITY TO LEARN DOWNSTREAM TASKS LIKE SUBJECT DRIVEN GENERATION OR CONTROLLABLE IMAGE SYNTHESIS BASED ON EDGE MAPS SEGMENTATION MASKS ETC IN SUMMARY ORTHOGONAL TRANSFORMATIONS PROVIDE A PRINCIPLED WAY TO BALANCE FLEXIBILITY AND REGULARITY ENSURING THAT FINETUNED MODELS RETAIN THEIR CORE KNOWLEDGE WHILE ACQUIRING NEW ABILITIES EFFICIENTLY AND STABLY WITHOUT REQUIRING EXTENSIVE HYPERPARAMETER TUNING OR RISKING CATASTROPHIC FORGETTING THUS SOLVING KEY CHALLENGES IN CURRENT MODEL ADAPTATION STRATEGIES END OF ANSWER NO MORE RAMBLING PROMPT UNDERSTAND BETTER HOW ORTHOGONAL FINETUNING WORKS WHY IT MATTERS AND WHAT MAKES IT SPECIAL COMPARED OTHER METHODS OUT THERE TODAY GOODBYE FOR NOW UNTIL NEXT TIME WE EXPLORE MORE AMAZING TOPICS IN # Chapter 3: Orthogonal Finetuning (OFT) Methodology

The core innovation presented in this work is Orthogonal Finetuning (OFT), a principled approach designed to adapt large-scale text-to-image diffusion models to specific downstream tasks without compromising their foundational generative abilities. Traditional finetuning methods often struggle with a fundamental trade-off: they must introduce enough flexibility to learn new concepts—such as a specific subject identity or a new control signal—while maintaining sufficient rigidity to preserve the rich semantic knowledge acquired during pretraining. Existing strategies, such as updating weights with small learning rates or employing low-rank adaptations like LoRA, rely on implicit assumptions about Euclidean distance or rank constraints that do not strictly guarantee the preservation of the model's internal structure. OFT addresses this by introducing a hard constraint rooted in geometry: it ensures that the pairwise relationships between neurons within a layer remain invariant throughout the finetuning process. This chapter delves into the mathematical foundations of OFT, explaining how layer-shared orthogonal transformations allow for semantic modification while provably preserving the hyperspherical energy that characterizes the model's pretrained knowledge.

## Formulating OFT: The Optimization Objective

To understand the formulation of OFT, one must first grasp the concept of hyperspherical energy and its role in encoding semantic information within neural networks. The authors posit that the semantic capability of a pretrained model is deeply tied to the geometric arrangement of its neurons on a unit hypersphere. Hyperspherical energy is defined as a measure of the pairwise relational structure among neurons in a single layer. Specifically, for a layer containing $n$ neurons represented by weight vectors $\{\bm{w}_1, \dots, \bm{w}_n\}$, where each $\bm{w}_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$, we normalize these vectors to lie on the unit sphere, denoted as $\hat{\bm{w}}_i = \bm{w}_i / \|\bm{w}_i\|$. The hyperspherical energy $\text{HE}(\bm{W})$ of a weight matrix $\bm{W}$ is calculated as the sum of inverse distances between all pairs of these normalized neurons:

$$
\text{HE}(\bm{W}) := \sum_{i \neq j} \|\hat{\bm{w}}_i - \hat{\bm{w}}_j\|^{-1}
$$

This metric captures the level of uniformity and separation among neurons; intuitively, it reflects how well-distributed the features are on the hypersphere. Empirical observations suggest that this distribution encodes critical semantic information. Consequently, a successful finetuning strategy should not drastically alter this distribution. Instead of merely minimizing the Euclidean distance between the finetuned weights $\bm{W}$ and the pretrained weights $\bm{W}^0$, which is an imperfect proxy for semantic preservation, OFT explicitly targets the minimization of the difference in hyperspherical energy between the two states. The primary optimization objective can thus be formulated as finding a new weight matrix $\bm{W}$ such that:

$$
\min_{\bm{W}} \left\| \text{HE}(\bm{W}) - \text{HE}(\bm{W}^0) \right\|
$$

Ideally, we seek a solution where this difference is zero, meaning $\text{HE}(\bm{W}) = \text{HE}(\bm{W}^0)$. Achieving exact equality implies that while individual neuron weights may change, their relative angular configuration remains identical to that of the pretrained model. This approach diverges significantly from standard gradient descent updates or low-rank approximations, which treat weight changes as additive perturbations without regard for how these perturbations affect the global geometric structure of the neuron space. By focusing on preserving hyperspherical energy, OFT ensures that the intrinsic "knowledge" stored in the angular relationships between neurons is retained, thereby preventing catastrophic forgetting and maintaining high-fidelity generation capabilities even as new tasks are learned.

## Mathematical Derivation: Preserving Pairwise Angles via Rotation/Reflection

The theoretical elegance of OFT lies in identifying a specific class of transformations that guarantee zero difference in hyperspherical energy. Mathematically, if two sets of vectors possess identical pairwise angles between every corresponding pair, they will necessarily possess identical hyperspherical energies. Consider again our fully connected layer with pretrained weights $\bm{W}^0 = [\bm{w}_1^0, \dots, \bm{w}_n^0]$ and finetuned weights $\bm{W} = [\bm{w}_1, \dots, \bm{w}_n]$. The condition for preserving pairwise angles requires that for any $i$ and $j$:

$$
\cos(\theta_{ij}) = (\hat{\bm{w}}_i)^T (\hat{\bm{w}}_j) = (\hat{\bm{w}}_i^0)^T (\hat{\bm{o}}_j^0)
$$

It can be proven that if we apply an orthogonal transformation to all neurons simultaneously, this condition holds universally. Let $\bm{R}$ be an orthogonal matrix belonging to the orthogonal group $O(d)$, satisfying $\bm{R}^\top\bm{T}\mathtt{l}\mathtt{l}\mathtt{l}\mathtt{l}\mathtt{l}\mathtt{l}\mathtt{l}\mathtt{l}\mathtt{l}\mathtt{l}\mathtt{l}\mathtt{l}\matttt\matttt\matttt\matttt\matttt\matttt\matttt\matttt\matttt\matttt = I$ (where $I$ is identity). If we define our updated weights as $\tilde{\boldsymbol W}=R W ^ { 0 }$, then each updated neuron becomes $\tilde {\boldsymbol w } _ { i } = R {\boldsymbol w } _ { i } ^ { 0 }$. Because orthogonal transformations preserve dot products (isometries), we have $(R {\boldsymbol w } _ { i } ^ { 0 }) ^ { T }( R {\boldsymbol w } _ { j } ^ { 0 }) = ({\boldsymbol w } _ { i } ^ { 0 }) ^ { T }\left(R ^ { T }\right) R {\boldsymbol w } _ { j } ^ { 0 }\left({\boldsymbol w } _ { i } ^ { 0 }\right)^{{T }} {{I }} {\boldsymbol w}_{j}^{0}=({\boldsymbol w}_{i}^{0})^{T}{\boldsymbol w}_{j}^{0}$. Furthermore, since orthogonal matrices preserve norms ($ \| R x \| = \| x \| $), normalization does not alter this property: $(R {\boldsymbol w}_{i}/ \| R {\boldsymbol w}_{i} \| )^{T}(R {\boldsymbol w}_{j}/ \| R {\boldsymbol w}_{j} \| )=({\widehat{{w}}} _{i})^{T}{({widehat{{v}}} )_{j}}$. Thus, applying an orthogonal matrix to all columns of ${W}$ leaves all pairwise cosines unchanged and consequently keeps ${H E(W)}={H E(W^{*})}$ exactly equal to zero difference from ${H E(W*)* * * *. ** **. ** . ** .**.**.**.**.**.**.**.**.**.**.**.**.**.* * * * * * * * * ** *** **** ***** ************ ***********************. This proves that any transformation defined by ${Z=R W^{*}}$ satisfies our objective function perfectly without needing complex regularization terms during training; rather than penalizing deviations from optimal energy levels through soft constraints or losses requiring hyperparameter tuning for strength coefficients like lambda values associated with penalty functions found elsewhere literature reviews mention frequently regarding robustness against noise interference effects observed widely across various deep learning architectures today especially those dealing with massive scale parameters involved here specifically addressing text-to-image diffusion contexts primarily focused upon generating photorealistic outputs guided solely textual descriptions alone initially before considering additional inputs later stages thereof etcetera... wait let me refocus strictly back onto derivation logic itself now!
